Wellposedness of the electron MHD without resistivity for large perturbations of the uniform magnetic field
In-Jee Jeong, Sung-Jin Oh

Abstract
. We prove the local wellposedness of the Cauchy problems for the electron magnetohydrodynamics equations (E-MHD) without resistivity for possibly large perturbations of nonzero uniform magnetic fields. While the local wellposedness problem for (E-MHD) has been extensively studied in the presence of resistivity (which provides dissipative effects), this seems to be the first such result without resistivity. (E-MHD) is a fluid description of plasma in small scales where the motion of electrons relative to ions is significant. Mathematically, it is a quasilinear dispersive equation with nondegenerate but nonelliptic second-order principal term. Our result significantly improves upon the straightforward adaptation of the classical work of Kenig–Ponce–Rolvung–Vega on the quasilinear ultrahyperbolic Schrödinger equations, as the regularity and decay assumptions on the initial data are greatly weakened to the level analogous to the recent work of Marzuola–Metcalfe–Tataru in the case of elliptic principal term. A key ingredient of our proof is a simple observation about the relationship between the size of a symbol and the operator norm of its quantization as a pseudodifferential operator when restricted to high frequencies. This allows us to localize the (non-classical) pseudodifferential renormalization operator considered by Kenig–Ponce–Rolvung–Vega, and produce instead a classical pseudodifferential renormalization operator. We furthermore incorporate the function space framework of Marzuola–Metcalfe–Tataru to the present case of nonelliptic principal term.



1.
Introduction

In the
(3+1)
-dimensional spacetime
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
, the
electron magnetohydrodynamics
system is given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}{\bf B}+\nabla\times((\nabla\times{\bf B})\times{\bf B})=0,\\
&\nabla\cdot{\bf B}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(E-MHD)

where
{\bf B}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a time-dependent vector field (magnetic field). The equation we consider is
without resistivity
, in the sense that it does not have the dissipative term
\mu\Delta{\bf B}
on the RHS of the first equation, and as a result, the energy
\frac{1}{2}\int|{{\bf B}(t)}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
is conserved. This equation is a fluid description of plasma in small scales, where the motion of electrons relative to ions is significant – which is typical in low plasma density and/or high temperature regimes, as argued in the work of Lighthill
[
52
]
where the model was introduced. It is also the simpler small-scale limit of Hall-MHD, which is another extended MHD model that takes the relative motion of electrons and ions into account (see equation (
Hall-MHD
) below). It is by now a well-established fact that (
E-MHD
) reproduces many essential features of the Hall-MHD system and (more accurate) two-fluid plasma models (see, e.g.,
[
58
]
).

The quadratic nonlinearity
\nabla\times((\nabla\times{\bf B})\times{\bf B})
is referred to as the
Hall current term
after the discovery of Hall
[
35
]
, and makes (
E-MHD
) a
quasilinear dispersive
equation. While the Hall current term is omitted in the usual ideal MHD model, it is known to be directly responsible for various situations including plasma confinement, collisionless magnetic reconnection featuring
X
-configuration (
[
6
,
67
,
38
,
27
]
), Hall-effect thrusters (
[
76
,
68
,
62
]
), planetary magnetospheres including that of the Earth’s (
[
26
,
57
]
) and magnetic field dynamics in neutron stars (
[
33
,
46
,
34
,
74
]
). In all of these situations, the effect of magnetic resistivity is considered negligible with respect to that of the Hall current term. Turbulence of electron-MHD system is a very active area of study in the plasma physics literature as well (
[
73
,
31
,
15
]
).

On the mathematical side, the illuminating work of Jang–Masmoudi
[
40
]
(see also
[
1
,
59
]
) formally derived the Hall-MHD equations (among other equations) from a two-species kinetic system consisting of electrons and ions, which clarifies that the Hall term is indeed a two-species effect. We shall review the existing mathematical literature on (
E-MHD
) in more detail below.

It is expected that a local wellposedness theory for (
E-MHD
) and related systems would be not only useful in rigorous derivation of MHD models but also provide a ground for a systematic study of various aforementioned physical phenomena involving the Hall effect. In the current work, we obtain the first local wellposedness result for (
E-MHD
) without resistivity, when the initial magnetic field is a (possibly large) perturbation of a nonzero uniform magnetic field, which we take to be
{\bf e}_{3}
(i.e., the uniform magnetic field of unit strength in the
x^{3}
-direction). This indeed seems to be the usual setup for (
E-MHD
) in the physics literature, including the original work of Lighthill (
[
52
]
). Furthermore, such an assumption cannot be completely avoided: in stark contrast to the resistive case, or even the usual ideal MHD model, the Cauchy problem for (
E-MHD
) and Hall-MHD is, in general, strongly illposed for arbitrarily small and smooth (even analytic) data (
[
44
]
). Analyzing the linearized equations near steady states with a degeneracy where
{\bf B}
vanishes, one sees that illposedness arises from
degenerate dispersion
, wherein the frequency of wave packets traveling towards degeneracy grows indefinitely in an arbitrarily short interval of time.

Our local wellposedness result is phrased in terms of the translation-invariant space introduced by Marzulola–Metcalfe–Tataru
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
in the context of quasilinear Schrödinger equations, but adapted to (
E-MHD
). To define this space, we first fix a partition
\mathcal{I}_{k}
of
\mathbb{R}
(for each nonnegative integer
k
) into intervals of length
2^{k}
, and a smooth partition of unity
\{\chi_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}
such that
\supp\chi_{I}\subseteq 2I
(i.e., the interval with the same center as
I
but twice the length). Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
u:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
, define

\|{u}\|_{\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s}}^{2}:=\sum_{k\geq 0}\left(2^{sk}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|{\chi_{I}(x^{3})P_{k}u}\|_{L^{2}}\right)^{2},

where
\{P_{k}\}
are the inhomogeneous Littlewood–Paley projections (see Section
2.1
). As in
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
, note that any translations of this norm are equivalent. But unlike in those papers, the variable
x^{3}
plays a distinguished role in the definition of our norm; this is because of the
conic directionality
of the group velocities for the linearization of (
E-MHD
) around
{\bf e}_{3}
, to be discussed in more detail below. As usual, we extend this norm to vector-valued functions by summing up the norm of all components.

Our main theorem may now be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.1

(Main theorem, simple version)
.

Let
s>\frac{7}{2}
and consider a vector field
{\bf B}_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfying
\nabla\cdot{\bf B}_{0}=0
. Assume furthermore that
{\bf B}_{0}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Nondegeneracy.

{\bf B}_{0}(x)\neq 0
at every point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

(2)

Asymptotic uniformity.

\|{{\bf B}_{0}-{\bf e}_{3}}\|_{\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s}}<+\infty
,

(3)

Nontrapping.
Every nonconstant solution
(X,\Xi)(t)
to the Hamiltonian system associated with
p_{{\bf B}_{0}}(x,\xi)={\bf B}_{0}(x)\cdot\xi|{\xi}|
escapes to
x^{3}=\pm\infty
, i.e.,
X^{3}(t)\to\infty
or
X^{3}(t)\to-\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

Then the Cauchy problem for (
E-MHD
) with
{\bf B}(t=0)={\bf B}_{0}
is locally wellposed.

The solution in Theorem
1.1
belongs to the space
{\bf B}-{\bf e}_{3}\in C([0,T);\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s})
for some
T>0
. To quantify the lifespan
T
guaranteed by our theorem, we need more quantitative information on
{\bf B}_{0}
. We refer the reader to Theorem
5.1
below for a more precise formulation. We shall see later in Corollary
5.2
that the nontrapping assumption is satisfied for small perturbations of
{\bf e}_{3}
, which gives the following:

Corollary 1.2

(Small data local wellposedness)
.

For a given
s>\frac{7}{2}
, there exist positive constants
\varepsilon,T
depending only on
s
such that for any
{\bf B}_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfying
\nabla\cdot{\bf B}_{0}=0
and
\|{{\bf B}_{0}-{\bf e}_{3}}\|_{\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s}}<\varepsilon
, there exists a unique local-in-time solution to (
E-MHD
) on the time interval
[0,T]
with initial data
{\bf B}_{0}
.

The motivation for the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
is as follows. Assumption (1) is a basic requirement that avoids, in particular, degenerate dispersion, which may lead to illposedness
[
44
]
. To motivate Assumption (2), as well as the distinguished role played by
x^{3}
in the definition of
\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s}
, let us consider the linearization (
E-MHD
) around
{\bf B}={\bf e}_{3}
, which is the constant-coefficient system

\partial_{t}b+{\bf e}_{3}\cdot\nabla(\nabla\times b)=0,\quad\nabla\cdot b=0.

Using the
L^{2}
-projections
\Pi_{\pm}(D)=
(where
D=i^{-1}\partial_{x}
and
m(D)
is the Fourier multiplier with symbol
m(\xi)
) to decompose
b=b_{+}+b_{-}
with
b_{\pm}=\Pi_{\pm}(D)b
, this system is diagonalized and we arrive at the equations

\partial_{t}b_{\pm}\pm{\bf e}_{3}\cdot D|{D}|b_{\pm}=0,

which is a dispersive equation with dispersion relation
\pm p(\xi)=\pm{\bf e}_{3}\cdot\xi|{\xi}|
. Hence, the group velocity takes the form

v_{\pm}(\xi):=\pm\nabla_{\xi}p(\xi)=\pm\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\xi_{1}\xi_{3}}{|{\xi}|}\\
\frac{\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}{|{\xi}|}\\
\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{3}+2\xi_{3}^{2}}{|{\xi}|}\end{pmatrix}.

Observe that the set of all possible group velocities form a cone around
{\bf e}_{3}
with aperture less than
\pi
– we call this the
conic directionality
of (
E-MHD
) (see also Lemma
4.1
). In particular, the
x^{3}
-component of the group velocity is always comparable to
|{\xi}|
, which is why the above definition of
\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s}
– involving localization and summation in only the
x^{3}
variable – is the suitable adaptation of the
l^{1}H^{s}
space introduced in
[
54
]
. As in
[
54
]
, the extra
\ell^{1}
summability controls the frequency evolution of wave packets in the far-away region – see also Lemmas
4.2
and
4.7
below.

If we consider instead the linearization of (
E-MHD
) around an arbitrary magnetic field
\mathring{{\bf B}}
, then the diagonalization of the principal symbol leads to the scalar symbols
\pm p_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}(t,x,\xi):=\pm\mathring{{\bf B}}(t,x)\cdot\xi|{\xi}|
, and the Hamiltonian flows associated to
\pm p_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
are the adequate generalization of the group velocities
v_{\pm}(\xi)
in the constant coefficient case. Assumption (3) ensures that, at least for a short time, there are no trapped bicharacteristics (i.e., solution to the Hamiltonian flow that stays in a bounded region for all times) with
\mathring{{\bf B}}={\bf B}
, which would be an obstruction for the
local smoothing effect
of (
E-MHD
). This effect is key to our proof, and will be discussed further in Section
1.1
below.

The system (
E-MHD
) may be compared with
quasilinear Schrödinger equations
, which are equations of the form

i\partial_{t}u+\sum_{j,k=1}^{d}\partial_{j}(g^{jk}(u,\overline{u},\nabla_{x}u,\nabla_{x}\overline{u})\partial_{k}u)=F(u,\overline{u},\nabla_{x}u,\nabla_{x}\overline{u})

(1.1)

where
g
and
F
are smooth functions of their variables, with
g
a real-valued symmetric matrix. In view of the nonellipticity of the principal symbol, (
E-MHD
) most closely resembles the
ultrahyperbolic
case, i.e., when
g
is nondegenerate but
not
positive definite.

Local wellposedness of the Cauchy problem for quasilinear ultrahyperbolic Schrödinger equations was first obtained in the landmark papers of Kenig–Ponce–Rolvung–Vega
[
47
,
48
]
. However, Theorem
1.1
significantly improves upon the straightforward adaptation of
[
47
,
48
]
, as the regularity and decay assumptions on the initial data are greatly weakened to the level analogous to the recent work of Marzuola–Metcalfe–Tataru
[
56
]
for the case when
g
is positive definite. Our approach to proving Theorem
1.1
builds upon these works, but also differs from both approaches in some key aspects; see Section
1.1
below.

Remark 1.3

(Extension to the Hall-MHD system)
.

In the absence of magnetic resistivity, the (incompressible) Hall-MHD system reads:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}{\bf B}+\nabla\times((\nabla\times{\bf B})\times{\bf B})=\nabla\times({\bf u}\times{\bf B}),\\
&\partial_{t}{\bf u}+{\bf u}\cdot\nabla{\bf u}+\nabla{\bf p}-\nu\Delta{\bf u}=(\nabla\times{\bf B})\times{\bf B},\\
&\nabla\cdot{\bf u}=\nabla\cdot{\bf B}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(Hall-MHD)

Here,
{\bf u},p
are the fluid velocity field and the pressure, respectively, and
\nu\geq 0
is the kinematic viscosity. As a simple extension of Theorem
1.1
, one can obtain the following result for (
Hall-MHD
): Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
for
{\bf B}_{0}
, if the initial velocity field
{\bf u}_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies
\nabla\cdot{\bf u}_{0}=0
and
{\bf u}_{0}\in\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s+1}
, then we have local wellposedness for (
Hall-MHD
) with initial data
({\bf B}_{0},{\bf u}_{0})
. We shall defer the precise statement and proof to our forthcoming work in
[
43
]
.

Remark 1.4

(Extension to quasilinear ultrahyperbolic Schrödinger equations
[
60
]
)
.

In view of the analogies with (
1.1
), a natural question is whether a result analogous to Theorem
1.1
can be also proved for (
1.1
) building on the ideas put forth in this paper. This is a nontrivial problem, as the conic directionality of
p_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
significantly simplifies the analysis of (
E-MHD
) compared to (
1.1
). Nevertheless, the answer to this question has been answered in
affirmative
; see the recent work of Pineau–Taylor
[
60
]
. See also Remark
1.6
below.

Remark 1.5

(Possible relaxations of assumptions)
.

Let us discuss some ways the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
may be relaxed. Some kind of nondegeneracy and nontrapping assumptions are always needed, but in view of the directionality of the bicharacteristics associated with
p_{{\bf B}_{0}}
, we may replace
\|{{\bf B}_{0}-{\bf e}_{3}}\|_{\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s}}<+\infty
by an assumption that does not require decay in the orthogonal direction to
x^{3}
. Moreover, we may let
{\bf B}_{0}
tend to different nondegenerate configurations as
x^{3}\to\pm\infty
. Some of these extensions will be explored in
[
43
]
.

The remainder of the introduction is structured as follows. In Section
1.1
, we describe the main ideas of our proof of Theorem
1.1
. In Section
1.2
, we discuss the previous literature on related problems. Then in Section
1.3
, an outline of the rest of the paper is provided.

1.1.
Main ideas

We now give a discussion of the main ideas of this paper.

1.1.1.
Basic setup and local smoothing estimate

A useful way to understand the nonlinear term
\nabla\times((\nabla\times{\bf B})\times{\bf B})
in (
E-MHD
) is to use paraproduct decomposition. To wit, we write (
E-MHD
) as

\partial_{t}{\bf B}-\nabla\times(T_{{\bf B}}\times(\nabla\times{\bf B}))+\nabla\times(T_{(\nabla\times{\bf B})}\times{\bf B})=(\cdots),

where
T_{{\bf A}}\times{\bf B}
is the
(low-high) paraproduct
defined (using Littlewood–Paley projections) as

T_{{\bf A}}\times{\bf B}:=\sum_{k}P_{<k-10}{\bf A}\times P_{k}{\bf B}.

The point of this decomposition is that the terms we omitted on the RHS, called
residual
or
high-high interaction
, are smoother (more precisely, see Proposition
5.5
). Moreover,
\nabla\times(T_{(\nabla\times{\bf B})}\times{\bf B})
on the LHS is less dangerous than the other term, since at most one derivative falls on the high frequency factor (namely,
{\bf B}
on the right). As in the previous discussion of the linearized (
E-MHD
) around
{\bf e}_{3}
, the first two terms on the LHS can be diagonalized (to the leading order) by introducing
b_{\pm}:=\Pi_{\pm}(D)b
where
b={\bf B}-{\bf e}_{3}
. We arrive at the system

{\bf L}_{{\bf B}}^{\sharp}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}:=\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}P_{{\bf B}}^{(2)\sharp}&0\\
0&-P_{{\bf B}}^{(2)\sharp}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathfrak{S}^{(1)\sharp}_{{\bf B}}\\
-\mathfrak{S}^{(1)\sharp}_{{\bf B}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}=(\cdots)

where
P_{{\bf B}}^{(2)\sharp}
is a diagonal anti-symmetric paradifferential (see Section
2.3
) second-order operator and
\mathfrak{S}_{{\bf B}}^{(1)\sharp}
is a matrix-valued symmetric paradifferential first-order operator given by

\displaystyle P_{{\bf B}}^{(2)\sharp}b

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k}\left[\frac{1}{2}{\bf B}^{\alpha}_{<k-10}P_{k}\partial_{\alpha}|{\nabla}|b+\frac{1}{2}|{\nabla}|\partial_{\alpha}P_{k}{\bf B}^{\alpha}_{<k-10}\right],

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{{\bf B}}^{(1)\sharp}b

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(|{\nabla}|(\partial^{\alpha}P_{<k-10}{\bf B}_{\beta})+(\partial_{\beta}P_{<k-10}{\bf B}^{\alpha})|{\nabla}|\right)P_{k}b^{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}[|{\nabla}|,P_{<k-10}{\bf B}\cdot\nabla]P_{k}b^{\alpha}\right],

and we have moved to the RHS and omitted less important terms for this heuristic discussion. See Sections
3
and
5
, as well as the proof of Proposition
7.2
, for details.
Terms in
(\cdots)
possibly loses
1
derivative of
b
, so we need to prove an estimate that gains
1
derivative for this system. This is precisely the role of the
local smoothing estimate

\displaystyle\|{\langle{D}\rangle^{s}\vec{b}}\|_{X^{0}}

\displaystyle:=\|{\langle{D}\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\vec{b}}\|_{LE}+\|{\langle{D}\rangle^{s}\vec{b}}\|_{L^{\infty}L^{2}[0,T]}

(1.2)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\langle{D}\rangle^{s}\vec{b}(t=0)}\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\langle{D}\rangle^{s}\vec{g}^{(1)}}\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}+\|{\langle{D}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\vec{g}^{(2)}}\|_{LE^{\ast}},

where
s>0
,
\vec{b}=(b_{+},b_{-})^{\top}
,
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}}^{\sharp}\vec{b}=\vec{g}^{(1)}+\vec{g}^{(2)}
and
LE
and
LE^{\ast}
are norms defined in (
2.1
)–(
2.2
) below. Comparing the terms involving
LE
and
LE^{\ast}
, one may note that this estimate indeed controls
1
more derivative of
\vec{b}
compared to
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}}^{\sharp}\vec{b}
. Heuristically, this order-
1
local smoothing phenomenon for (
E-MHD
) is expected based on the nondegeneracy, asymptotic uniformity and nontrapping properties of
{\bf B}
(see Theorem
1.1
); see also the discussion below.

For the ensuing discussion, let us also introduce the (non-paradifferential) linear operator

{\bf L}_{{\bf B}}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}:=\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}P_{{\bf B}}^{(2)}&0\\
0&-P_{{\bf B}}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{{\bf B}}\\
-\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{{\bf B}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}

where

\displaystyle P_{{\bf B}}^{(2)}b

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}{\bf B}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}|{\nabla}|b+\frac{1}{2}|{\nabla}|\partial_{\alpha}{\bf B}^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{{\bf B}}^{(1)}b

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(|{\nabla}|(\partial^{\alpha}{\bf B}_{\beta})+(\partial_{\beta}{\bf B}^{\alpha})|{\nabla}|\right)b^{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}[|{\nabla}|,{\bf B}\cdot\nabla]b^{\alpha},

which is a part of the linearization of (
E-MHD
) around
{\bf B}
(see Section
3
).

1.1.2.
Previous approaches

Having set up a more concrete setting for a heuristic discussion, let us now review the previous approaches to local wellposedness of quasilinear dispersive equations. Indeed, in the pioneering papers of Kenig–Ponce–Vega
[
49
,
50
,
51
]
and Kenig–Ponce–Rolvung–Vega
[
47
,
48
]
, which proved local wellposedness of quasilinear Schrödinger equations with elliptic and ultrahyperbolic principal terms, respectively, the main idea was to establish local smoothing estimates for suitable class of variable coefficient linear dispersive equations (see also
[
53
,
14
,
37
]
for earlier works). Translated to our context
1
1

1

In particular,
b_{+}
and
b_{-}
in our context are analogous to
u
and
\bar{u}
, respectively, in
[
47
]
. The symbol for the renormalization operator – referred to as an “integrating factor” – is denoted by
K
.
, the class of equations they considered consists of non-paradifferential operators
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}_{0}}
around a time-independent magnetic field
{\bf B}_{0}
– we note that
{\bf B}_{0}
may be taken to be the initial magnetic field (hence our notation), since we expect
{\bf B}
to stay close to the initial data (in an adequate sense) on the time interval under consideration. The proof of local smoothing estimate for
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}_{0}}
according to
[
47
]
(the ultrahyperbolic case, which is closer to (
E-MHD
)) would proceed as follows:

Step 1: Local smoothing assuming boundedness of energy.
Establish (
1.2
) for
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}_{0}}\vec{b}=\vec{g}^{(1)}+\vec{g}^{(2)}
assuming

\|{\vec{b}}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}[0,T]}\lesssim\|{\vec{b}(t=0)}\|_{H^{s}}+\|{\vec{g}^{(1)}}\|_{L^{1}H^{s}}+\|{\langle{D}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\vec{g}^{(2)}}\|_{LE^{\ast}},

(1.3)

Step 2: Boundedness of energy.
Establish (
1.3
) for
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}_{0}}\vec{b}=\vec{g}^{(1)}+\vec{g}^{(2)}
.

The first step involves a
positive commutator argument
, based on the geometric observation that every bicharacteristic
(X(t),\Xi(t))
associated with
{\bf B}_{0}\cdot\xi|{\xi}|
initially in a slab of the form
\{\alpha<x^{3}<\beta\}
exits the slab at time at most
O(\frac{\beta-\alpha}{|{\Xi(0)}|})
(during which
|{\Xi(t)}|
stays comparable to
\Xi(0)
) under suitable nondegeneracy, asymptotic uniformity (i.e., decay) and nontrapping assumptions (as in Theorem
1.1
). For the second step, we start by writing out the equations for
\tilde{b}_{\pm}:=\langle{D}\rangle^{s}b_{\pm}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{b}_{+}\\
\tilde{b}_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\left[\begin{pmatrix}P_{{\bf B}_{0}}^{(2)}&0\\
0&-P_{{\bf B}_{0}}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{C}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}&0\\
0&-\mathfrak{C}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}\\
-\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}&0\end{pmatrix}\right]\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{b}_{+}\\
\tilde{b}_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\langle{D}\rangle^{s}{\bf L}_{{\bf B}}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+(\cdots),

where
\mathfrak{C}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}:=[\langle{D}\rangle^{s},P_{{\bf B}_{0}}^{(2)}]\langle{D}\rangle^{-s}
is a diagonal
symmetric
first-order operator arises from commuting
\langle{D}\rangle^{s}
with
P_{{\bf B}_{0}}^{(2)}
. Then we control the time derivative of the
H^{s}
-energy
\|{\tilde{b}_{+}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\tilde{b}_{-}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
by multiplying the above equation on the left by
(\tilde{b}_{+},\tilde{b}_{-})
and integrating on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The key difficulty here is that
\mathfrak{C}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}
contributes a term involving one derivative of
\tilde{b}_{\pm}
without smallness
(if
{\bf B}_{0}-{\bf e}_{3}
is large), and hence which cannot be directly handled with local smoothing in Step 1 without circular reasoning.

The idea of
[
47
]
(a formal version of this idea goes back to
[
16
,
25
,
37
]
) was to remove the problematic term
\pm\mathfrak{C}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}\tilde{b}_{\pm}
by working with a conjugation
\Op(O)\tilde{b}_{\pm}
, where
\Op(O)
is a suitable pseudodifferential operator (see Section
2.3
for the notation). A short formal computation tells us that the symbol of
O
needs to satisfy

\mathrm{H}_{{\bf B}_{0}}O(x,\xi)=\mathfrak{c}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}(x,\xi)O(x,\xi),

(1.4)

where
\mathrm{H}_{{\bf B}_{0}}
is the vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
(geometrically,
T^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{3}
) associated with the Hamiltonian flow of
{\bf B}_{0}(x)\cdot\xi|{\xi}|
and
\mathfrak{c}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}(x,\xi)
is the principal symbol of
\mathfrak{C}^{s(1)}_{{\bf B}_{0}}
. The good news is that such a symbol
O(x,\xi)
is uniformly bounded under the assumptions on
{\bf B}_{0}
in Theorem
1.1
. The bad news, however, is that the derivatives of
O(x,\xi)
behave badly. In fact,
O(x,\xi)
does
not
belong to the classical symbol class
S^{0}
, but rather only obeys

|{\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\partial_{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}O(x,\xi)}|\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}\langle{x}\rangle^{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|}|{\xi}|^{-|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|},

which is a nonstandard symbol class considered by Craig–Kappeler–Strauss
[
16
]
. In particular, it is known that the above assumptions are
not
sufficient to guarantee the boundedness of the operator
\Op(O)
in
L^{2}
. Kenig–Ponce–Vega–Rolvung
[
47
]
show, nevertheless, that
O(x,\xi)
, after suitable modifications in the construction, belongs to a refined (but still nonstandard) class of symbols for which
L^{2}
-boundedness of
\Op(O)
and a suitable version of symbolic calculus hold. The operator
\Op(O)
is then used to carry out the proof of boundedness of energy, thereby completing the proof of local smoothing. The argument in
[
47
]
requires strong regularity and decay assumptions on
{\bf B}_{0}
(in particular, compared to Theorem
1.1
).

Recently, Marzuola–Metcalfe–Tataru
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
revisited the local wellposedness problem for quasilinear Schrödinger equations and greatly improved the hypotheses on regularity and decay of initial data. Some of the ideas introduced in
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
are: working with a paralinearization instead of the usual linearization of the equation (i.e.,
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}}^{\sharp}
instead of
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}_{0}}
), introduction of a function space framework that encodes the necessary decay in a translation-invariant manner (see
\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}H^{s}
) and a new proof of local smoothing estimate that, in particular, does not involve the renormalization operators
\Op(O)
. However, the method of
[
56
]
(the large data case) cannot be directly applied to (
E-MHD
), as it uses the ellipticity of the principal term in an important manner.

1.1.3.
New ideas in this work

In this work, we introduce a new approach that builds upon – but is distinct from – both
[
47
]
and
[
54
]
. A key new ingredient in our proof is a simple observation, which is of interest on its own, that we dub the
high frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt trick
(see Lemma
2.10
for details):

For
a\in S^{0}
(classical symbol class), the
L^{2}\to L^{2}
-operator norm of
Op(a)
restricted to inputs supported in high frequency (i.e.,
\|{Op(a)P_{>k}(D)}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
) is bounded by
\|{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,\xi}}
, provided that the frequency threshold
k
is large enough (depending on higher order bounds for
a
).

This trick is useful since, in the problem of local wellposedness, a low frequency part of the solution is easy to deal with using the energy estimate and the smallness of the time interval.

Our approach for proving local smoothing for
{\bf L}_{{\bf B}_{0}}
follows the basic roadmap of
[
47
]
sketched above, but using the high frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt trick we get enough quantitative precision to perform a
physical space localization
of the renormalization operator so that (i) it lies in the
classical
symbol class
S^{0}
instead of an nonstandard class as in
[
47
]
, and (ii) weaker regularity and decay assumptions for
{\bf B}_{0}
as in Theorem
1.1
are sufficient. Concretely, our localized renormalization operator
O_{\pm}
for
\tilde{b}_{\pm}
takes the form

\displaystyle O_{\pm}(x,\xi)=\begin{cases}O(x,\xi)&|{x^{3}}|\lesssim R,\\
\exp(\pm C_{O})&x^{3}\gtrsim R\\
\exp(\mp C_{O})&-x^{3}\gtrsim R,\end{cases},

where
C_{O}>0
is some constant (independent of
R
);
R
is chosen sufficiently large so that
{\bf B}_{0}
is small in
\{x^{3}\gtrsim R\}
, which we quantify by
\epsilon
;
O
solves (
1.4
); and
O_{\pm}(x,\xi)
is chosen to be (suitably) monotonic along bicharacteristics in the transition regions
|{x^{3}}|\simeq R
, which is crucial for the localization to be admissible (which, in turn, is why we let
O_{\pm}
depend on
\pm
). The error resulting from this localization is bounded by
C\epsilon\|{O_{\pm}\tilde{b}_{\pm}}\|_{X^{0}}
, which we must absorb using the local smoothing estimate in Step 1.

Since
O_{\pm}(x,\xi)
is constant in
|{x^{3}}|\gtrsim R
, we have
O_{\pm}(x,\xi)\in S^{0}
. But the symbol bounds depend on
R
, so the operator norm of
\Op(O_{\pm})
will still depend on
R
2
2

2

To make matters worse, in reality the derivatives of
O_{\pm}
also depend on the maximal length
L
of bicharacteristics inside
\{|{x^{3}}|\lesssim R\}
(see Definition
4.3
). But this issue is resolved similarly to the
R
-dependence issue discussed here via the high frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt trick.
. At this point, one may worry about running into a circular reasoning in the choice of
\epsilon
and
R
unless
R
is quantitatively related to
\epsilon
(e.g., there is a strong decay assumption on
{\bf B}_{0}
analogous to
[
47
]
). Our key observation, however, is that
\|{O_{\pm}}\|_{L^{\infty}}
is bounded
independently
of
\epsilon
and
R
(essentially since
O
is) and hence, by the high frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt trick (more precisely, its strengthening in Proposition
6.3
),
\|{\Op(O_{\pm})P_{>k_{(1)}}}\|_{X^{0}\to X^{0}}
is also bounded
independently
of
R
for
k_{(1)}
sufficiently large. As remarked above, the contribution of the low frequencies
P_{<k_{(1)}}
can be treated via simpler energy estimates by choosing the time
T
small
3
3

3

The frequency threshold
k_{(1)}
depends on
R
, but it is compensated by choosing
T
small at the end of the argument.
. This observation breaks the possible circularity and allows us to prove boundedness of energy with only a translation-invariant decay assumption on
{\bf B}_{0}
. Moreover, as we avoid introducing any nonstandard symbol classes, our renormalization argument is significantly simpler than
[
47
]
. See Section
7.5
for the detailed argument
4
4

4

Although, pedantically, Section
7.5
considers the paralinearized system, which is more difficult than the simplified problem discussed here.
.

In fact, we are able to carry out our argument (essentially) in the context of
paralinearized operator

{\bf L}_{{\bf B}}^{\sharp}
(see Section
7
for the precise setting) and in the function space framework analogous to
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
(see Section
2.2
and Section
6
). As a result, we optimize the regularity and decay assumptions on the initial data to the same level as
[
56
]
.

Remark 1.6

(Extension to quasilinear ultrahyperbolic Schrödinger equations
[
60
]
)
.

The conic directionality of (
E-MHD
) simplifies our argument, as evinced by the form of
O_{\pm}(x,\xi)
above (in particular, it suffices to localize the symbol to the slab
\{|x^{3}|\lesssim R\}
independent of
\xi
). In a recent work
[
60
]
of Pineau–Taylor, the strategy of physical space localization via high frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt trick has been extended to the quasilinear ultrahyperbolic Schrödinger equations (
1.1
), where the conic directionality no longer holds and further new ideas are required.

1.2.
Discussions

Whistler waves versus Alfvén waves
. We make a comparison between Whistler and Alfvén waves, where the latter is the basic wave for the usual ideal MHD system. The ideal MHD system linearized around
(0,{\bf e}_{3})
reads

\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\bf b}+{\bf e}_{3}\cdot\nabla{\bf u}=0,\qquad\partial_{t}{\bf u}={\bf e}_{3}\cdot\nabla{\bf b},\end{split}

and this can be written as

\begin{split}\partial_{tt}{\bf b}+({\bf e}_{3}\cdot\nabla)^{2}{\bf b}=0,\end{split}

which is nothing but the (1+1)-dimensional wave equation in
(t,x^{3})
. Therefore, we see that the Alfvén waves travel along
{\bf B}
with speed
|{{\bf B}}|
, while Whistler waves travels within a cone around
{\bf B}
with speed proportional to
|{{\bf B}}||{\xi}|
. It is this property of Whistler waves that is responsible for various aforementioned phenomena observed in plasmas.

Mathematical literature on Hall and electron MHD.
Regarding Hall and electron MHD equations with magnetic resistivity, local well-posedness and temporal decay was obtained in pioneering works
[
7
]
and
[
10
]
, respectively. Most of the subsequent mathematical literature dealt with the resistive case as well (
[
9
,
17
,
24
,
12
,
75
,
77
,
72
,
71
,
23
,
18
,
19
]
), and the simpler case of
2\frac{1}{2}
-dimensional solutions have been widely studied (
[
5
,
61
,
75
,
13
,
29
,
21
]
), not only by mathematicians but also by physicists. This means that one considers magnetic fields of the form

\begin{split}{\bf B}=\nabla\times(\psi\,{\bf e}_{3})+\phi\,{\bf e}_{3},\end{split}

(1.5)

where
\psi,\phi
are scalar valued functions of
t,x^{1},x^{2}
. In this case, (
E-MHD
) reduces to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\psi+\nabla^{\perp}\phi\cdot\nabla\psi=0,&\\
\partial_{t}\phi-\nabla^{\perp}\psi\cdot\nabla\Delta\psi=0.&\end{aligned}\right.

(1.6)

(Here,
\nabla^{\perp}=(\partial_{x^{1}},\partial_{x^{2}})
and
\nabla^{\perp}=(-\partial_{x^{2}},\partial_{x^{1}})
. In the presence of the velocity field, a similar ansatz can be given for it as well.) Recently, Dai
[
21
]
added a
\Delta\phi
term to the RHS of the
\phi
equation in (
1.6
) and obtained global wellposedness of smooth solutions near the uniform magnetic field
{\bf B}={\bf e}_{1}
. In the same paper, it is conjectured that global regularity persists when (
1.6
) has a resistivity term only in the
\psi
equation. Moreover, we note that dyadic models for Hall- and electron-MHD systems have been studied in
[
20
]
and
[
22
]
.

In the irresistive case, Chae–Weng
[
11
]
presented smooth axisymmetric solutions of Hall- and electron-MHD equations which blow up in finite time (see also
[
28
,
30
,
41
]
).
5
5

5

This was done without having
uniqueness
corresponding to the initial data, which is still an open problem.
However, for general smooth initial data, strong illposedness statements including
nonexistence
of smooth solutions to Hall- and electron-MHD were proved in
[
44
]
, by considering degenerate magnetic fields under the
2\frac{1}{2}
-dimensional setup. That is, for certain
C^{\infty}
-smooth and decaying initial data to (
1.6
), there cannot be no associated smooth solution in any arbitrarily short interval of time. Similar illposedness statements for smooth data compactly supported in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for (
E-MHD
) and (
Hall-MHD
) will be handled in our forthcoming work
[
42
]
.

Degenerate quasilinear dispersive PDE.

The nondegeneracy condition of the principal term was assumed in the aforementioned works on quasilinear dispersive equations. (This is the condition (1) in the statement of Theorem
1.1
in our case.) When the principal term has a point of degeneracy, analysis of the bicharacteristic curves near such a point shows a very rapid growth of the frequency (for instance, see
[
32
,
45
,
2
,
4
,
3
]
) in general. An argument involving generalized energy identities and testing against wavepackets introduced in
[
44
]
provides a robust way of establishing norm growth of the solutions to quasilinear degenerate dispersive PDE corresponding to the behavior of bicharacteristics. This argument was then generalized to give strong illposedness for various families of degenerate quasilinear dispersive PDE in
[
45
,
8
]
, including degenerate versions of KdV and Schrödinger equations. For some of these equations, local wellposedness (not in Sobolev or Hölder-type spaces, but rather in a space adapted to the degeneracy) for certain classes of degenerate initial data has been obtained in
[
32
,
36
]
based on the Lagrangian approach. Motivations for studying degenerate dispersive equations can be found for instance in
[
32
,
64
,
63
,
66
,
65
]
.

1.3.
Structure of the paper

In
Section 2
, we collect some preliminaries for the whole paper, including notation and conventions (Section
2.1
), a precise formulation of the function space framework (Section
2.2
), review of pseudodifferential and paradifferential operators (Sections
2.3
), and the high frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt trick (Section
2.4
). In
Section
3
, we record some core computations of this paper, namely a reformulation of (
E-MHD
) as an equation for a perturbation
b
of a background magnetic field and also the introduction of the variables
b_{\pm}
, with respect to which the principal term in the linearized E-MHD is diagonalized. In
Section
4
, we study the properties of the Hamiltonian flow associated with the principal symbol of the linearized system after diagonalization. Moreover, a quantitative formulation of the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
is given in this section (Definition
4.3
). In
Section
5
, we give a precise version of the main theorem (Theorem
5.1
) and reduce its proof to a local smoothing estimate for the paralinearized system (Proposition
5.4
) and nonlinear estimates for the remainder (Proposition
5.5
). Then, after proving some relevant linear and nonlinear estimates in our function space framework in
Section
6
, we prove Propositions
5.4
and
5.5
in
Section
7
, which is the technical heart of our paper.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation and conventions

We use the following notation in the paper.

•

\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
denotes the set of nonnegative integers.

•

\langle{\cdot}\rangle=(1+(\cdot)^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
(\cdot)_{+}=\max\{\cdot,0\}
.

•

\mathbf{1}_{E}
: characteristic function of
E

•

\chi_{>1}(\cdot),\chi_{>R}(\cdot)
:
\chi_{>1}(\cdot)
is a smooth even function such that
\chi_{>1}(s)=1
for
|{s}|>1
,
\chi_{>1}(s)=0
for
|{s}|<\frac{1}{2}
and nondecreasing on
(0,\infty)
.
\chi_{>R}(\cdot)=\chi_{>1}(\tfrac{\cdot}{R})

•

\chi_{<1}(\cdot),\chi_{<R}(\cdot)
:
\chi_{<1}(\cdot)
is a smooth even function such that
\chi_{<1}(s)=1
for
|{s}|<1
,
\chi_{<1}(s)=0
for
|{s}|>2
and nonincreasing on
(0,\infty)
.
\chi_{<R}(\cdot)=\chi_{<1}(\tfrac{\cdot}{R})

Vector calculus and index notation

•

We use the vector calculus notation

\nabla f=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}f\\
\partial_{2}f\\
\partial_{3}f\end{pmatrix},\quad\nabla^{\top}f=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}f&\partial_{2}f&\partial_{3}f\end{pmatrix},\quad\nabla\times{\bf u}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{2}{\bf u}_{3}-\partial_{3}{\bf u}^{2}\\
-\partial_{1}{\bf u}^{3}+\partial_{3}{\bf u}^{1}\\
\partial_{1}{\bf u}^{2}-\partial_{2}{\bf u}^{1}\end{pmatrix}.

•

We also employ the index notation, where the plain greek letters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots
are used for vector indices. As usual, repeated upper and lower indices are always summed (unless otherwise specified), and we raise and lower indices using the Kronecker delta
\mathchoice{\delta^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}
(which is
1
if
\alpha=\beta
and
0
otherwise). For computation, it will sometimes be useful to express a usual vector calculus operation in the index notation. For instance, the curl is expressed as

(\nabla\times{\bf u})^{\alpha}=(\nabla\times)^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf u}^{\beta}=\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{{\alpha\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.6714pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.05101pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\alpha\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.6714pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.05101pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\alpha\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.6714pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.05101pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\alpha\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.6714pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.05101pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}\partial_{\gamma}{\bf u}^{\beta},

where
\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}
is the Levi-Civita symbol (totally antisymmetric with
\epsilon_{123}=1
).

•

We use the boldfaced greek letters
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\ldots
for multi-indices.

Fourier analysis and Littlewood–Paley theory

•

Given a function
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we write
m(D)
for the Fourier multiplier operator with multiplier
m(\xi)
;
D=i^{-1}\nabla
. For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
|{\nabla}|^{\sigma}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}
.

•

In this paper, we consider the inhomogeneous Littlewood–Paley projections with respect to the spatial frequency. Fix some
\phi_{0}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-negative, decreasing, smooth function supported on
[0,2]
, which satisfies
\phi_{0}(\xi)=1
for
|\xi|\leq 1
. For
k\geq 1
, set
\phi_{k}(\xi)=\phi_{0}(2^{-k}\xi)-\phi_{0}(2^{-k+1}\xi)
, and then we define
P_{k}
to be the Fourier multiplier operator with multiplier
\phi_{k}(|\xi|)
for each
k\geq 0
. We also write
P_{<k}=\sum_{k^{\prime}<k}P_{k^{\prime}}
.
P_{[k,k^{\prime})}=P_{<k^{\prime}}-P_{<k}
. Since we use the inhomogeneous projections, it is assumed that the summation with respect to
k,k^{\prime},\cdots
is only for nonnegative integers.

2.2.
Function spaces

Let
J
be a time interval. Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
refers to a partition of
\mathbb{R}
into intervals of size
2^{\ell}
, and that
\{\chi_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}}
is a smooth partition of unity such that
\supp\chi_{I}\subseteq 2I
. Note that for
I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
, either
I\cap I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
or
\dist(I,I^{\prime})\gtrsim 2^{\ell}
.

The local smoothing (or local energy decay) norms are:

\|{b}\|_{LE[J]}=\sup_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}}2^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\|{u}\|_{L^{2}L^{2}(J\times\{x^{3}\in I\})}

(2.1)

\|{g}\|_{LE^{\ast}[J]}=\inf_{g=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}g_{\ell}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}}2^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\|{g_{\ell}}\|_{L^{2}L^{2}(J\times\{x^{3}\in I\})}

(2.2)

Then for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we define the dyadic norms (i.e., a family of norms indexed by
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
) by

\|{b}\|_{X_{k}[J]}=2^{\frac{k}{2}}\|{b}\|_{LE[J]}+\|{b}\|_{L^{\infty}L^{2}[J]}

(2.3)

\|{g}\|_{Y_{k}[J]}=\inf_{g=g_{1}+g_{2}}\left(2^{-\frac{k}{2}}\|{g_{1}}\|_{LE^{\ast}[J]}+\|{g_{2}}\|_{L^{1}L^{2}[J]}\right).

(2.4)

Following
[
54
]
, given any dyadic norm
\mathcal{X}_{k}
and
r\in[1,\infty]
, we define

\displaystyle\|{b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{X}_{k}[J]}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|{\chi_{I}(x^{3})b}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}[J]}^{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}},

(2.5)

and for
r=\infty
, we replace the summation in
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
by the supremum as usual.

These dyadic spaces are slowly varying in the following sense:

Lemma 2.1

(Slow variance of
X_{k}
and
Y_{k}
)
.

For any
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
1\leq r\leq\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\|{b}\|_{X_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{1}{2}|{k-k^{\prime}}|}\|{b}\|_{X_{k^{\prime}}},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\|{b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}X_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{3}{2}|{k-k^{\prime}}|}\|{b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}X_{k^{\prime}}},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\|{g}\|_{Y_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{1}{2}|{k-k^{\prime}}|}\|{g}\|_{Y_{k^{\prime}}},

(2.8)

\displaystyle\|{g}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}Y_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{3}{2}|{k-k^{\prime}}|}\|{g}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}Y_{k^{\prime}}}.

(2.9)

Moreover, the following embedding properties hold:

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle\|{b}\|_{\ell^{\infty}_{\mathcal{I}}X_{k}}\lesssim\|{b}\|_{X_{k}}\lesssim\|{b}\|_{\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}X_{k}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|{g}\|_{\ell^{\infty}_{\mathcal{I}}Y_{k}}\lesssim\|{g}\|_{Y_{k}}\lesssim\|{g}\|_{\ell^{1}_{\mathcal{I}}Y_{k}}.

(2.11)

We omit the proofs of Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
, which follow rather immediately from the definitions.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the summed-up spaces by

\displaystyle\|{b}\|_{X^{s}[J]}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(2^{sk}\|{P_{k}b}\|_{X_{k}[J]})^{2},

\displaystyle\|{g}\|_{Y^{s}[J]}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(2^{sk}\|{P_{k}g}\|_{Y_{k}[J]})^{2},

\displaystyle\|{b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}X^{s}[J]}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(2^{sk}\|{P_{k}b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}X_{k}[J]})^{2},

\displaystyle\|{g}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}Y^{s}[J]}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(2^{sk}\|{P_{k}g}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}Y_{k}[J]})^{2},

and so on. For
s>\frac{7}{2}
and
r\in[1,\infty]
, observe that the following string of embeddings hold:

\displaystyle\ell^{r}X^{s}[J]\hookrightarrow\ell^{r}L^{\infty}H^{s}[J]\hookrightarrow\ell^{r}C_{t}C^{2,s-\frac{7}{2}}_{x}[J]\hookrightarrow C_{t}C^{2,s-\frac{7}{2}}_{x}[J],

where the third embedding is Morrey’s inequality. Observe also that the following bound holds for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in[1,\infty]
:

\|{b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}X^{s}[J]}\lesssim\|{b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}L^{\infty}H^{s}[J]}+|{J}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|{b}\|_{\ell^{r}_{\mathcal{I}}L^{\infty}H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}[J]}.

(2.12)

We omit the proofs of these straightforward bounds.

2.3.
Pseudo- and paradifferential operators

In this subsection, we review the basic theory of
pseudo-
and
paradifferential operators
that will be used in this paper.

2.3.1.
Classical symbols and quantizations

Let
\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)
be a complex-valued smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We say that
\mathfrak{a}
is a (scalar-valued)
classical symbol of order
m

(m\in\mathbb{R})
, and write
\mathfrak{a}\in S^{m}
, if

[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}:|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\leq N}\sup_{x,\xi}|{\langle{\xi}\rangle^{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|{-m}}\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\partial_{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)}|<+\infty\hbox{ for every }N\geq 0.

(2.13)

We also write
S^{-\infty}=\cap_{m\in\mathbb{R}}S^{m}
.
Given a symbol
\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\in S^{m}
, the corresponding
left
(resp.
right
)
quantization
is formally
6
6

6

The formal expressions of the form
\iint(\cdots)e^{i\xi\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\xi
in this section may be made precise by multiplying the integrand by
m_{\leq 0}(\epsilon x)m_{\leq 0}(\epsilon\xi)
and taking
\epsilon\to 0
, thanks to the regularity bounds on the integrand originating from the classical symbol bounds. We omit the standard details.
defined as

\displaystyle\Op^{(l)}(\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi))u=\mathfrak{a}(x,D)u

\displaystyle=\iint\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)u(y)e^{i\xi\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{(2\pi)^{d}},

\displaystyle\Big(\hbox{resp.}~\Op^{(r)}(\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi))u=\mathfrak{a}(D,x)u

\displaystyle=\iint\mathfrak{a}(y,\xi)u(y)e^{i\xi\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Big)

for
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since we will mainly use left quantization, and often simply write
\Op(\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi))=\Op^{(l)}(\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi))
. Note that if
a(x,\xi)
splits into
a(x,\xi)=c(x)m(\xi)
, then
c(x)
is multiplied to the left (resp. right) of
m(D)
under left (resp. right) quantization, i.e.,

\Op(\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi))=\Op^{(l)}(\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi))=c(x)m(D),\quad\Op^{(r)}(\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)=m(D)c(x).

(2.14)

The following basic
L^{2}
boundedness and Gårding’s inequality are standard:

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in S^{0}
.

(1)

(
L^{2}
-boundedness) We have

\|{\mathfrak{a}(x,D)}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{0};10d}.

(2)

(Gårding’s inequality) If, in addition,
\mathfrak{a}
is real-valued and
\mathfrak{a}\geq 0
, then

\langle{\mathfrak{a}(x,D)u,u}\rangle\geq-C[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{0};20d}\|{u}\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

The same result holds for
\mathfrak{a}(D,x)
.

For a proof, see
[
70
, Thm. 0.5.C]
and
[
70
, Prop. 0.7.A]
.
However, in this paper (especially in Section
7
), we will need a more precise understanding of the dependence of
\|{\mathfrak{a}(x,D)}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
on the symbol bounds. For this purpose, we will rely on the Calderón–Vaillancourt theorem, which is a more powerful
L^{2}
-boundedness theorem; see Section
2.4
below.

2.3.2.
Paradifferential operators

To work in the low-regularity translation-invariant setting analogous to Marzuola–Metcalfe–Tataru
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
, we also employ
paradifferential operators
. Since we also need to consider the composition and commutation of such operators with classical pseudodifferential operators, it will be beneficial to review some of their theory. A more systematic account of paradifferential operators can be found in, e.g.,
[
70
]
.

Adopting the notation in
[
70
]
, we define the symbol class
\mathcal{B}S^{m}_{1,1}
as

\mathcal{B}S^{m}_{1,1}:=\{\mathfrak{a}:[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m}_{1,1};N}<+\infty\hbox{ for all }N\geq 0,\,\supp\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}(\eta,\xi)\subseteq\{|{\eta}|<\tfrac{1}{8}|{\xi}|\}\},

where (formally)
\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}(\eta,\xi)=\int\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)e^{-i\eta\cdot x}\,\mathrm{d}x
and

[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m}_{1,1};N}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}:|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\leq N}\sup_{x,\xi}|{\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|-{m}}\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\partial_{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)}|.

An important example of a paradifferential operator is the
paraproduct
, defined as follows: given
g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the paraproduct of
g
and
u
is

T_{g}u=\sum_{k}P_{<k-10}gP_{k}u,

or equivalently,
T_{g}=\Op(\sum_{k}P_{<k-10}g(x)P_{k}(\xi))
. Clearly,
T_{g}\in\mathcal{B}S^{0}_{1,1}
with

\Big[\sum_{k}P_{<k-10}g(x)P_{k}(\xi)\Big]_{S^{0}_{1,1};N}\lesssim_{N}\|{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Another important way that paradifferential operators arise is by the following decomposition. Given
\mathfrak{a}\in S^{m}
, consider the decomposition
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}^{\sharp}+\mathfrak{a}^{\flat}
, where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{\sharp}(x,\xi)=\sum_{k}P_{<k-10}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)P_{k}(\xi),\quad\mathfrak{a}^{\flat}(x,\xi)=\sum_{k}P_{\geq k-10}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)P_{k}(\xi).

where
P_{<k-10}
and
P_{\geq k-10}
acts on the
x
-variable. It is easy to verify that (for any
N,m_{0}\geq 0
)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{B}S^{m}_{1,1}\hbox{ with }[\mathfrak{a}^{\sharp}]_{S^{m}_{1,1};N}\leq[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{\flat}

\displaystyle\in S^{-\infty}\hbox{ with }[\mathfrak{a}^{\flat}]_{S^{m-m_{0}}_{1,1};N}\lesssim_{m,m_{0},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+m_{0}}.

(2.16)

We have the following basic
L^{p}
boundedness theorem for paradifferential operators.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{B}S^{0}_{1,1}
. Then for every
1<p<\infty
, we have

\|{\mathfrak{a}(x,D)}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}\lesssim[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{0}_{1,1};10d}.

The same result holds for
\mathfrak{a}(D,x)
.

For a proof, see
[
70
, Prop. 3.4.F]
.

2.3.3.
Symbolic calculus for pseudodifferential operators

We now recall the symbolic calculus for pseudodifferential operators. In our argument, we need to consider composition (and commutation) of classical pseudodifferential operators and paradifferential operators. Here, we record precise formulas for the remainders.

We begin with composition. Let
\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)
and
\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)
be symbols that are either in
S^{m}
or
\mathcal{B}S^{m}_{1,1}
(with possibly different orders
m
). Then the composition
\mathfrak{a}(x,D)\mathfrak{b}(x,D)
is again a left quantization of a classical symbol, i.e.,

\mathfrak{a}(x,D)\mathfrak{b}(x,D)=(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,D)

where
(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)
is formally given by

(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)=e^{-i\xi\cdot x}\mathfrak{a}(x,D)\mathfrak{b}(x,D)e^{i\xi\cdot(\cdot)}=\iint\mathfrak{a}(x,\eta)\mathfrak{b}(y,\xi)e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}.

By Taylor expansion, we have

\begin{split}\mathfrak{a}(x,\eta)&=\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)(\eta-\xi)_{a}(\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a})(x,s\eta+(1-s)\xi)\,\mathrm{d}s\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}(x,\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)+(\eta-\xi)_{a}(\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a})(x,\xi)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)(\eta-\xi)_{a}(\eta-\xi)_{b}(\partial_{\xi_{a}}\partial_{\xi_{b}}\mathfrak{a})(x,s\eta+(1-s)\xi)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Using
(\eta-\xi)_{a}e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}=-i^{-1}\partial_{y^{a}}e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}
, integrating
\partial_{y^{a}}
by parts and noting that
\int e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}=\delta_{0}(x-y)
, we obtain the formal formulae

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)

(2.17)

\displaystyle=-\iint\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)(\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a})(x,s\eta+(1-s)\xi)(\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{b})(y,\xi)e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}

and

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)-i^{-1}\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)

(2.18)

\displaystyle=-\iint\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)(\partial_{\xi_{a}}\partial_{\xi_{b}}\mathfrak{a})(x,s\eta+(1-s)\xi)(\partial_{x^{a}}\partial_{x^{b}}\mathfrak{b})(y,\xi)e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}.

Based on these, the following result can be established.

Proposition 2.5

(Composition of pseudodifferential operators)
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in S^{m}
.

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{b}\in S^{m^{\prime}}
. Then

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\in S^{m+m^{\prime}},

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\in S^{m+m^{\prime}-1},

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)-i^{-1}\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\in S^{m+m^{\prime}-2},

with

\displaystyle[(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}[\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}};N+10d},

\displaystyle[(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-1};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}[\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}};N+10d},

\displaystyle[(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)-i^{-1}\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-2};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}[\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}};N+10d}.

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1}
and

\supp\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(\eta,\xi)\subseteq\{|{\eta}|<\tfrac{1}{16}|{\xi}|\}.

(2.19)

Then

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{1,1}+S^{-\infty}.

More precisely, the expression decomposes into
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\sharp}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{1,1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\flat}\in S^{-\infty}
, where

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\sharp}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{1,1};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}[\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d},

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\flat}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-1+m_{0}};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},m_{0},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+m_{0}+10d}[\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d},

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)-i^{-1}\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-2}_{1,1}+S^{-\infty}.

More precisely, the expression decomposes into
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\sharp}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-2}_{1,1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\flat}\in S^{-\infty}
, where

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\sharp}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-2}_{1,1};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}[\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d},

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\flat}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-1+m_{0}};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},m_{0},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+m_{0}+10d}[\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d}.

(3)

If
\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1}
for
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\leq 1
and (
2.19
) holds, then

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{1,1}+S^{-\infty}.

More precisely, the expression decomposes into
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\sharp}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{1,1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\flat}\in S^{-\infty}
, where

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\sharp}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{1,1};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}:|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=1}[\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d},

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-1)\flat}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-1+m_{0}};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},m_{0},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+m_{0}+10d}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}:|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=1}[\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d},

Moreover, if
\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1}
for
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\leq 2
and (
2.19
) holds, then

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)-i^{-1}\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-2}_{1,1}+S^{-\infty},

More precisely, the expression decomposes into
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\sharp}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m+m^{\prime}-2}_{1,1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\flat}\in S^{-\infty}
, where

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\sharp}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-2}_{1,1};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}:|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=2}[\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d},

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{r}^{(m+m^{\prime}-2)\flat}]_{S^{m+m^{\prime}-1+m_{0}};N}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,m^{\prime},m_{0},N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+m_{0}+10d}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}:|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=2}[\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m^{\prime}}_{1,1};N+10d}.

Statement (1) is standard (see, e.g.
[
70
, Prop. 0.3.C]
), while Statements (2) and (3) follow essentially from
[
70
, Proof of Thm. 3.4.A]
. These may also be easily obtained by studying the explicit remainder (
2.18
) using stationary phase.

Proposition
2.5
has immediate consequences for commutators and Poisson brackets. Given two symbols
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
, recall that the commutator is
[\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{a}]=\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b}
and the Poisson bracket is defined by the symbol
\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\}=(\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)-\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{b}(x,\xi)\partial_{x^{a}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi))
. Then, as a consequence of Proposition
2.5
(1), we have the following statement.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in S^{m}
and
\mathfrak{b}\in S^{m^{\prime}}
be classical symbols. Then, we have that

\begin{split}[\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}],\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\}\in S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}[\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}]-\frac{1}{i}\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\}\in S^{m+m^{\prime}-2}.\end{split}

For later use, it will be convenient to record the following specific case of Proposition
2.5
(2)–(3), when one of the symbols takes the form of a paraproduct.

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}
be a classical symbol and
T_{g}=\sum_{k}P_{<k-10}gP_{k}
. Then, we have

\begin{split}\|{[\mathfrak{a},T_{g}]}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{1};10d}\|{g}\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\end{split}

(2.20)

and

\begin{split}\|{[\mathfrak{a},T_{g}]-\frac{1}{i}\Op\{\mathfrak{a},T_{g}\}}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{2};10d}\|{g}\|_{W^{2,\infty}}.\end{split}

(2.21)

Next, we discuss the adjoint operation. Given a classical symbol
\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)
, it is not difficult to verify that

\mathfrak{a}(x,D)^{\ast}=\mathfrak{a}^{\dagger}(D,x).

This simple relation motivates the question of computing the symbol of a right quantization
\mathfrak{a}(D,x)
as a left-quantized pseudodifferential operator. We have

\mathfrak{a}(D,x)=\mathfrak{a}^{r\to\ell}(x,D),

where
\mathfrak{a}^{r\to\ell}(x,\xi)
is formally given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{r\to\ell}(x,\xi)=e^{-i\xi\cdot x}\mathfrak{a}(D,x)e^{i\xi\cdot(\cdot)}=\iint\mathfrak{a}(y,\eta)e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}.

Using the expansion
\mathfrak{a}(y,\eta)=\mathfrak{a}(y,\xi)+\int_{0}^{1}(\eta-\xi)^{\alpha}(\partial_{\xi_{\alpha}}\mathfrak{a})(y,s\eta+(1-s)\xi)\,\mathrm{d}s

and proceeding as in the derivation of (
2.18
), we obtain the formal formula

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{r\to\ell}(x,\xi)-\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)=\iint\int_{0}^{1}i^{-1}(\partial_{x^{a}}\partial_{\xi_{a}}\mathfrak{a})(y,s\eta+(1-s)\xi)e^{i(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y)}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}.

(2.22)

Proposition 2.8

(Right to left quantization)
.

The following statements hold.

(1)

If
\mathfrak{a}\in S^{m}
, then
\mathfrak{a}^{r\to\ell}-\mathfrak{a}\in S^{m-1}
, with

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{a}^{r\to\ell}-\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m-1};N}\lesssim_{m,N}[\mathfrak{a}]_{S^{m};N+10d}.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m}_{1,1}
with (
2.19
), then
\mathfrak{b}^{r\to\ell}-\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{B}S^{m-1}_{1,1}
, with

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{b}^{r\to\ell}-\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m-1}_{1,1};N}\lesssim_{m,N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}:|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=1}[\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathfrak{b}]_{S^{m}_{1,1};N+10d}.

Statement (1) is standard; see, e.g.,
[
70
, Prop. 0.3.B]
. Statement (2) again follows essentially from
[
70
, Proof of Thm. 3.4.A]
. It may also be easily obtained by studying the explicit remainder (
2.22
) using stationary phase.

Although we will not need them in the sequel, we note that analogous formulas where the left and right quantizations are swapped can be derived in a similar manner.

2.4.
L^{2}
-boundedness, positivity and high frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt trick

We recall the Calderón–Vaillancourt theorem, which is a key
L^{2}
-boundedness theorem for pseudodifferential operators.

Theorem 2.9

(Calderón–Vaillancourt)
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy

|{\nabla_{x}^{(n^{\prime})}\nabla_{\xi}^{(n)}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)}|\leq 1\quad\hbox{ for }|{n}|+|{n^{\prime}}|\leq 10d.

(2.23)

Then
\mathfrak{a}(x,D)
is bounded from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with the norm bounded by a universal constant.

An important difference of this statement from Proposition
2.3
(1) is that
\mathfrak{a}
is not required to belong to the classical symbol class
S^{m}
. We refer to
[
69
]
for a proof.

Consider now a smooth matrix-valued symbol
\mathfrak{a}
obeying
7
7

7

One may of course formulate (
2.24
) and Lemma
2.10
setting
A=B=1
without losing any generality. However, we choose the current formulation as we often work with symbols that behave in a regular way when differentiated in
x
or in
\xi
as in (
2.24
).

|{\partial_{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\partial_{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)}|\leq c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\beta}}\lambda^{-|{\beta}|},\quad\supp\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\{(x,\xi):\frac{1}{100}\lambda<|{\xi}|<100\lambda\},

(2.24)

for some constants
c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.

Of course, qualitatively,
\mathfrak{a}\in S^{0}
, so the standard
L^{2}
boundedness theorem and sharp Gårding’s inequality apply. However, such results do not give precise constants in relation to
\lambda
, and hence are insufficient for our paper. Nevertheless, it turns out that the operator norm of
\Op(\mathfrak{a})
(in, say,
L^{2}
) can be directly bounded by the size
c_{00}
of the symbol
\mathfrak{a}
, as long as we restrict the input to sufficiently high frequencies (which may depend on
c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\beta}}
with
(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})\neq(0,0)
). The proof is by a simple scaling argument combined with Theorem
2.9
.

Lemma 2.10

(High-frequency Calderón–Vaillancourt)
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}
be a smooth symbol satisfying (
2.24
) for
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\leq 100d
. If

\lambda\geq\max_{1\leq|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\leq 10d}\left(\frac{c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{c_{00}}\right)^{\frac{2}{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|}},

(2.25)

then, for some universal constant
C>0
,

\|{\mathfrak{a}(x,D)}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq Cc_{00}.

(2.26)

Proof.

Let us consider a rescaling
\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of the symbol
\mathfrak{a}
of the form

\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}(x,\xi)=c_{00}^{-1}\mathfrak{a}(\lambda_{0}^{-1}x,\lambda_{0}\xi).

If we take
\lambda_{0}=\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then

|{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}(x,\xi)}|\leq\frac{c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{c_{00}}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|}{2}}.

Thus, if (
2.25
) holds, then
\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}
obeys the hypothesis of Theorem
2.9
. Since
\|{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}(x,D)}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}=c_{00}^{-1}\|{\mathfrak{a}(x,D)}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
, we see that
\|{\mathfrak{a}(x,D)}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq Cc_{00}
as desired. ∎

3.
Reformulation of (
E-MHD
)

3.1.
Reformulation of (
E-MHD
) for perturbations of
\mathring{{\bf B}}

Given a background magnetic field
\mathring{{\bf B}}
with
\nabla\cdot\mathring{{\bf B}}=0
, which may not necessarily be a solution to (
E-MHD
), consider the decomposition of a solution
{\bf B}
to (
E-MHD
) into
\mathring{{\bf B}}
and perturbation
b
:

{\bf B}=\mathring{{\bf B}}+b.

In terms of
b
, (
E-MHD
) may be reformulated in the following way:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}b+\nabla\times((\nabla\times b)\times(\mathring{{\bf B}}+b))+\nabla\times((\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}})\times b)=-\partial_{t}\mathring{{\bf B}}-\mathcal{P}(\mathring{{\bf B}})\\
&\nabla\cdot b=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.1)

where
\mathcal{P}(\mathring{{\bf B}})
is the nonlinearity of (
E-MHD
):

\mathcal{P}(\mathring{{\bf B}}):=\nabla\times((\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}})\times\mathring{{\bf B}})

We call (
3.1
) the
perturbation equation around the background magnetic field
\mathring{{\bf B}}
. Note that the RHS vanishes if
\mathring{{\bf B}}
solves (
E-MHD
). In this case, keeping only the linear part in
b
, we arrive at the
linearized (
E-MHD
) around the background solution
\mathring{{\bf B}}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}b+\nabla\times((\nabla\times b)\times\mathring{{\bf B}})+\nabla\times((\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}})\times b)=0,\\
&\nabla\cdot b=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.2)

Motivated by (
3.2
), we introduce the following shorthand for the spatial part of the linearized equation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}b=

\displaystyle\nabla\times((\nabla\times b)\times\mathring{{\bf B}})+\nabla\times((\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}})\times b).

(3.3)

We also introduce the decomposition
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
, where

\mathcal{P}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}b=\nabla\times((\nabla\times b)\times\mathring{{\bf B}}),\quad\mathcal{Q}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}b=\nabla\times((\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}})\times b).

(3.4)

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
contains the principal term, while
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
is lower order.

3.2.
Diagonalization of the principal symbol

Replacing
\nabla
by the symbol
i\xi
in the term
\mathcal{P}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}b
in (
3.4
), we see that the principal symbol of the linearized (
E-MHD
) around
\mathring{{\bf B}}
(i.e., (
3.2
)) takes the form

\mathchoice{({\bf p}_{\mathring{{\bf {\bf p}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
. In what follows, we use the shorthand

. Note that

\displaystyle=

By simple linear algebra, we see that if we define

criptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\gamma\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.6714pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.05101pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\gamma\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.39928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.6714pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.05101pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}\xi_{\gamma}}{i|{\xi}|}-\frac{\xi^{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}}{|{\xi}|^{2}}\right),

then

\Pi_{s}^{2}=\Pi_{s},\quad\Pi_{s}\Pi_{s^{\prime}}=0,\quad s,s^{\prime}\in\{-,0,+\},

and
\Pi_{-}+\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{+}=I
. Moreover,

\displaystyle{\bf p}(x,\xi)=

\displaystyle ip(x,\xi)\Pi_{+}(\xi)-ip(x,\xi)\Pi_{-}(\xi),

\displaystyle p(x,\xi)=

\displaystyle p_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}(x,\xi):=\mathring{{\bf B}}^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}|{\xi}|,

which is the desired diagonalization of
{\bf p}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
. We will refer to
p(x,\xi)=p_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}(x,\xi)
as the
scalar principal symbol
of (
3.2
).

Remark 3.1
.

The matrices
\Pi_{+}(\xi)
,
\Pi_{0}(\xi)
,
\Pi_{-}(\xi)
are nothing but eigenspace projections of the cross product
\xi\times
viewed as an operator on
\mathbb{C}^{3}(=\mathbb{R}^{3}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})
. Accordingly, the corresponding multipliers
\Pi_{+}(D)
,
\Pi_{0}(D)
and
\Pi_{-}(D)
are
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
projections that diagonalize the curl operator
\nabla\times
. Since

\mathchoice{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}(\xi)=\overline{\mathchoice{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}(-\xi)},\qquad s\in\{-,0,+\},

it follows that
\Pi_{s}(D):L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

3.3.
Linearized equation after diagonalization of the principal part

Motivated by the computation in Section
3.2
, we return to the linearized (
E-MHD
) equation (
3.2
),

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}b+\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}b=g,\\
&\nabla\cdot b=0,\end{aligned}\right.

and compute the equation satisfied by the diagonalized variables
b_{\pm}=\Pi_{\pm}(D)b
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The system (
3.2
) is equivalent to the following system for the diagonalized variables
b_{\pm}=\Pi_{\pm}(D)b
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}b_{\pm}\pm P_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}^{(2)}b_{\pm}\pm\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}b_{\mp}\pm\mathfrak{A}^{(1)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}b_{\pm}\pm\nabla\left(\mathfrak{U}^{(0)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}(b_{\pm}+b_{\mp})\right)

(3.5)

\displaystyle+(\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}})\cdot\nabla b_{\pm}+\mathfrak{R}^{(0)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}};\pm}(b_{\pm}+b_{\mp})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\pm}(D)g,

or in the form of a system,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}P_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}^{(2)}&0\\
0&-P_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}\\
-\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}

(3.6)

\displaystyle+\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{A}^{(1)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}&0\\
0&-\mathfrak{A}^{(1)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+(\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}})\cdot\nabla\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle+\nabla\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{U}^{(0)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}&\mathfrak{U}^{(0)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}\\
-\mathfrak{U}^{(0)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}&-\mathfrak{U}^{(0)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{R}^{(0)}_{\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}};+}&\mathfrak{R}^{(0)}_{\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}};+}\\
\mathfrak{R}^{(0)}_{\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}};-}&\mathfrak{R}^{(0)}_{\nabla\times\mathring{{\bf B}};-}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b_{+}\\
b_{-}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{+}(D)g\\
\Pi_{-}(D)g\end{pmatrix},

where
P^{(2)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
is the scalar 2nd order operator of the form

P^{(2)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}=\frac{1}{2}\left((\mathring{{\bf B}}\cdot\nabla)|{\nabla}|+|{\nabla}|(\mathring{{\bf B}}\cdot\nabla)\right),

\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}_{\mathring{{\bf B}}}
is a matrix-valued symmetric 1st order operator of the form
